# W1.4 dogfood — real-network `border scan` on residue-capable packages

Date: 2026-09-10 · HEAD: 7579856 + W1.4 polish (crates envelope forensic log) · binary: local `dist/index.js` (esbuild bundle, `npm run build` RC0)
Harness: every scan run from an empty scratch cwd under /tmp via `timeout 180 node <dist>/index.js scan <spec>`, stdout/stderr captured separately, exit code recorded verbatim.

| spec | attempted | actual | exit | findings | top rules |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `puppeteer@23.11.1` | 23.11.1 (exists, no fallback) | 23.11.1 | 1 | 1 | `lifecycle-script` (native, CRITICAL) |
| `playwright@1.55.0` | 1.55.0 (exists, no fallback) | 1.55.0 | 1 | 347 | `path-pattern:\\\\[^\\]+\\` ×339, `path-pattern:/home/[a-z]+/` ×4, `path-pattern:[A-Za-z]:\\` ×4 (secretlint) |
| `pypi:nltk@3.9.1` | 3.9.1 (exists, HTTP 200, no fallback) | 3.9.1 | 2 | 0 | — (fail-closed exit 2, see below) |

All three pinned versions resolved on the first attempt — no 404 fallback was needed.

## Interpretation — is each output actionable triage?

### puppeteer@23.11.1 — YES, sharp single finding (exit 1)

The lifecycle-script gate fired exactly as the plan predicted: puppeteer's
`postinstall: node install.mjs` is surfaced as ONE CRITICAL blocking finding
naming the hook, its body, and the doctrine ("npm executes it on every
consumer install"). For a human reviewer this is immediately actionable:
installing this package runs code. The T2 download behavior (Chrome binaries)
is attributed to that hook — the scan never executes it, which is the point:
the finding says "a hook runs", the uninstall roundtrip valve (0.4.0) is what
would prove what it persists.

Observations (flag only, rule text NOT edited):
- The message embeds the internal glossary id "(G33)" — opaque to an external
  reviewer; consider expanding or dropping it.
- Because the gate CRITICAL-blocks the rebuild leg, the deeper source-level
  residue profile of puppeteer (what `install.mjs` actually does) is not in
  the report. That is fail-closed-by-design, but the output never says so — a
  reader may think the package has only one finding when the scan was
  truncated by its own guard. A one-line "rebuild leg suppressed by lifecycle
  gate" note would make the verdict self-explanatory.

### playwright@1.55.0 — verdict correct, triage quality POOR (exit 1, 347 CRITICAL)

Two honest facts first: (a) playwright@1.55.0's package.json has NO lifecycle
scripts at all (verified via `npm view playwright@1.55.0` → `scripts: None`,
deps `playwright-core@1.55.0`; browser download became opt-in ≥1.51), so the
absence of a `lifecycle-script` finding is truthful, not a miss; the actual
T2 downloader lives in `playwright-core`, which scan deliberately did not
fetch (scan inspects the requested artifact, not its dependency closure).
(b) the exit-1 FAIL verdict is stable across reruns.

The 347 findings, however, do NOT read as actionable triage:

- ~96% of them (333) sit inside four VENDORED, MINIFIED third-party bundles
  (`lib/mcpBundleImpl.js` 106, `lib/transform/babelBundleImpl.js` 83,
  `lib/common/expectBundleImpl.js` 81, `lib/utilsBundleImpl.js` 63). The
  secretlint path-pattern rules match escape-sequence noise in minified JS
  (`\\x0…`, `C:\`-shaped fragments inside regex/string literals), not real
  Windows-UNC/home-directory references. A human would need to triage or
  suppress 333 vendor-bundle false positives to reach the 14 first-party hits.
- ALL 347 are CRITICAL → severity carries no prioritization signal here.
- The context shown is truncated to `▮▮▮▮` or `\x0…="[\` after redaction, and
  no line numbers appear — several findings are byte-identical duplicates
  (4× the same line for `types/test.d.ts`), so a reviewer cannot even tell
  which match each row refers to.
- The rule id IS the raw regex (`path-pattern:\\\\[^\\]+\\`), which reads as
  garbled escaping rather than a diagnostic label.
- Message shape "found matching path-pattern:<regex>: <snippet>" is nearly
  tautological (rule id repeated inside the message).

Flagged as observations (NOT fixed per W1.4 scope): per-file aggregation or a
vendor/minified-file discount for secretlint path patterns, non-CRITICAL
severity for regex-shaped matches, and dedup of identical (rule, path, line)
rows would each materially improve triage.

### pypi:nltk@3.9.1 — clean fail-closed (exit 2), NOT a scan bug — STOPPED probing per W1.4 protocol

Stdout empty, stderr one line, exit 2: no fake "clean" was printed, matching
the doctrine "a scan that cannot genuinely run never prints a clean verdict".
Root cause verified against the artifact itself (independent re-download,
sha256 `87d127bd3de4bd89a4f81265e5fa59cb1b199b27440175370f7417d2bc7ae868`
matching PyPI's declared digest, size 2904691): the nltk 3.9.1 sdist ships
ONLY `setup.py` + `setup.cfg` — there is NO `pyproject.toml` anywhere in the
968-entry archive. The pypi channel builds from `[build-system]`, so rejecting
a legacy setuptools sdist is correct-by-design; the pypi dogfood leg therefore
exercises fetch+materialize (which accepted setup.py) and the channel's
fail-closed boundary, but not a full pypi rebuild.

Observations (flag only):
- The error is wrapped as `border: unexpected error: no pyproject.toml …` —
  the "unexpected error" label makes a legitimate domain rejection look like
  an internal crash; exit 2 is right, the wording is misleading.
- `materializePackage` accepts `setup.py`-only pypi trees (its manifest
  contract) while the pypi stage then rejects them — two different notions of
  "valid pypi package" in one pipeline; the user-visible effect is a scan that
  does fetch, extract, commit, and only THEN refuses. Cheap early rejection
  would save ~2 registry downloads.

### crates envelope forensic log (W1.4 polish D1)

Not exercised by these three npm/pypi specs (no `.crate` fetched); the new
per-file `border scan: crates envelope normalized: <file> sha256=<hex>`
stderr records are covered by `test/scan.materialize.test.ts` (tests 3–4:
exact hash strings, absent-file silence, real-stderr default sink). RED→GREEN
proof lives in the W1.4 DONECLAIM.
